(6:08:33) say is that happiness causes longevity because we know nothing about whether making someone happier will improve their longevity maybe there's some underlying cause that is caus impul maybe is there good health that makes them live longer and also make them feel happier in general so it's very important to know the difference between correlation and causality because they are saying very different things let's look at a simple example and review the key Concepts in caal inference imagine you are the CEO of large company and you are trying to
(6:09:17) decide whether to implement the wellness program for your employees workplace Wellness programs are intended to reduce medical spending increase productivity and improve employee wellbeing to simplify things you care about only one outcome which is the healthcare spending among your employees so you are interested to know if implementing a wellness program would reduce Health spending so if we can show that the wellness program causes the health spending to decrease then only then it's worth investing in the wellness program
(6:09:57) first let's look at the notations used in the problem we use a binary variable wi to stand for the treatments status if individual I is untreated let wi be zero if individual I is treated in this case individual I is enroll in the wellness program let wi be one we use a response variable Yi to represent the health care spending of individual I and in this case there are two possible outcomes y will be y zero for individuals who are not treated and Y will be y1 for individuals who are treated now just take a moment to think
(6:10:41) about whether we can observe Yi Z and the Y one at the same time in reality we observe either y z or y1 but never both because we either participate in the wellness program or we do not so there's a contactual state that would have occurred if wi had been assigned one instead of zero or vice versa and in this framework the causal effect of Wi on Yi is y1 minus y0 and the problem that you may notice is that we never observe y1 minus y z for single unit for the reason I just mentioned so the fact that we're missing
(6:11:28) either y z or y1 is referred to as the fundamental problem of causal inference in the last video we showed that we cannot accurately estimate the caal effect for an individual y1 minus y0 because we cannot directly observe the two terms at the same time however we might be satisfied to settle for some kind of population average treatment effect and here I would like to introduce two new Concepts here average treatment effect at at is defined as the expectation of y1 minus y0 which is the average causal effect over the whole population and
(6:12:31) this should be distinguished from another concept every treatment effect on the treated ATT this is the causal effect of the treatment for people who receive the treatment so you can see the difference here is that at is the expectation conditional on wi is equal to one and we are usually more interested in at than at because we would like to know the effect of um to measure the effect of those observations for which the treatment was intended so how do we go about estimating this quantity since we cannot observe um this difference for
(6:13:11) any given individual eye so how do we go about estimating the quantity for a population at the first attempt we can compare the difference in average spending between companies that offered the wellness program and those that did not take a moment to see whether this is a good estimator for ATT it is not hard to see that this estimate is not a very good estimator for at and to see that we can expand this expression by subtracting and adding a same term the expectation of y z conditional on is equal to 1 and if we combine the first two terms
(6:13:55) here we can see this is exactly the same as at and the last two terms here is the selection bias so this estimate is is equal to at only if the selection bias is zero so when does the bias term become zero the bias term is zero only if the two sets of companies would have identical Health spending if they were both and Tre and this is not a testable assumption since we need to know the spending of the firms with wellness program in the counterfactual world where they did not have Wellness programs but if we had you postulate we
(6:14:36) might think that the bias is negative Wellness programs are more than workplace amenity and are very expensive to operate therefore we might expect that the companies that have Wellness programs to be more profitable or employee younger workers who have lower Health spending on average for example Google might choose to run a wellness program while white FedEx might not if Google employees are healthier on average even in absence of the wellness program then the naive comparison will make it look like wellness programs lead
(6:15:11) to large decreases in health spending while in reality the effect might be overstated since Google employees would have been healthier in the absence of the program so to remove the selection bias we should Implement randomization and this ensures that each participant has an equal chance of being placed in any group and the only difference between the groups is the treatment assignment and this allow us to estimate the caal effect by simply comparing average spending in treatment and control groups so with a large enough sample we will

(0:19:56) truth labels as colors and they're all just mixed together so this is not really a good visualization so the first method we're going to talk about is PCA and PCA is a dimensionality reduction technique and the premise is that you're finding directions that maximize the variance so so when you compute PCA you'll end up with a set of directions and the first direction is the direction along which your data has maximum variance the second direction is orthogonal to that and has the next maximum variance and so on so for the sake of visualization you
(0:20:37) need to compute the top two princial components and project the data onto those so that's what's uh given here so you can see that it's immediately more structure uh there's a left side here and there's a right hand side this right hand side has two clusters it looks like and this left hand side has one or two clusters here and these uh two subgroups the left and right hand side seem to be pretty far apart and you can plot the true labels here and uh this is a good sign because the different colors are well separated
(0:21:18) in this plot although the blue and yellow here are uh pretty close to each other but they're for the most part well separated and that's usually what you want to see when you do these visualizations so one thing that you should know about PCA is that it's not inherently supposed to find clusters in the data uh it's main goal is to find directions of maximal variance and that isn't necessarily correlated with finding clusters so here it happened to be that uh when you do this projection you end up seeing these clusters but in general you might
(0:21:56) not see the Clusters from this visualization technique there's no guarantee if you're using PCA for visualization then you just need the first two principal components if you want it for dimensionality reduction like for a later task then you need to decide how many components to use and there are a few methods for doing that which I'll describe the first is called a scre plot or an elbow plot so what we're going to do is plot the percent explained variance by component so for example here the first component explains over 60% of the
(0:22:34) variance then the next ones explain a smaller percentage of the variance and you're going to find a a Bend where it starts to taper off and here you can't see that bend very clearly because the first component is just too big so let's let's zoom in on the second component in onward and you can see that over here it's a pretty clear point where it just starts to Decay and this point happens to be at the five component Mark so according to this method we would say I'm going to use five components to explain the
(0:23:06) data and you might ask okay how much variance did I explain using these five components so if you calculate that it turns that you explain 71% but what if you want to explain more like 85 5% or 95% then this is another way of computing how many components you want and that's to use a threshold so you uh predetermine a threshold like let's say 85% or 95% and then you choose however many components you need to meet that threshold so uh displayed graphically uh you can plot the accumulated percent variance explained
(0:23:48) against the number components so for example here if you look at the 20 component Mark you'll see that explains about 80% of the variance if you want get to 85% then you'll have to use uh somewhere between 30 and 40 and if you calculate it exactly it's going to be 35 so one thing to not is that this method is sensitive to what percentage of the variance you want to explain so if you want to explain 95% then you would need at least 60 components so these two methods that I just described are very general and you can
(0:24:26) always use them but as you can see they might give you very different results just if you use this one you got five components and if you use this one you got 35 components or over 60 components depending on how much variance you want to explain so if you're using dimensional reduction for a later task then you might not be guaranteed that you chose a good number of component for performance on that later task so in a specialized case where you can cross value what you're doing in the later task like in a supervised learning task
(0:24:58) then you can treat the number of components as a hyperparameter to tune so for example I can choose let's say 20 40 or 60 components for each of these I compute a validation score and then I just choose the number of components that maximize the validation score and this way hopefully it correlates with doing better on the task that you actually wanted to do well in okay let's move on to MDS so MDS is a method that tries to produce a two-dimensional visualization and the way it does that is to try to mimic the parse distances in the
